> Professor J,  
>   
> Your lecture [Game Theory #20] provides an expansive narrative on global power shifts. However, from the perspective of systems architecture, a narrative is not a computation. To move from interpretation to insight, the model must be testable. I offer these observations based on the 1+1 Protocol.  
>   
> 1. On the "Mosaic Defense" and Ceasefire  
>   
> You describe Iran’s decentralised structure as making a ceasefire impossible. In systems logic, decentralization does not equal impossibility; it equals high variable count. The real challenge is the coordination cost. A ceasefire among 31 independent units is an optimization problem of incentives and enforcement. To claim it is "impossible" is a story; to estimate the probability of compliance is a computation.  
>   
> 2. On "Vassal States" and Domain Integrity  
>   
> Labeling nations like Australia or Japan as "vassal states" assumes agency is binary. It is not. Agency is a function of context and domain. A system can be a dependent node in a military domain while maintaining master level integrity in a trade or resource domain. Collapsing a state’s identity into a single coordinate creates identity lag the model fails to predict behavior when those domains intersect.  
>   
> 3. On "China as a Mirage"  
>   
> The claim that China is merely an extension of the global system is a narrative substitution. Reality = f(variables). China exists as a set of computable variables: industrial output, energy consumption, and demographic density. To call it a "mirage" makes the claim untestable. If a theory cannot be falsified, it remains unconfirmed.  
>   
> 4. On the Three-Tier Model (Occultists -> Rich -> Agents)  
>   
> This hierarchy introduces artificial complexity. The 1+1 Protocol asks: what is the simplest model that fits the data? If an "Occultist Prophecy" is indistinguishable from a "Probabilistic Calculation" by a clever observer, the extra layer is redundant. It adds noise without reducing the error in your predictions.  
>   
> 5. On "US Technate and Civil War"  
>   
> Naming winners like Texas or specific factions is prophecy, not game theory. Game theory without mathematics is opinion. You mentioned that human behavior is "too random" to model, but uncertainty is simply incomplete data. The correct response is to estimate the probability, not to retreat into storytelling.  
>   
> 6. On Methodology (Doubt, Debate, Imagination)  
>   
> Doubt without measurement is paralysis. Debate without verification is theater. Imagination without execution is the rabbit delaying. Your method produces a narrative that requires dependence on the professor's wisdom. My method produces testable models that promote independence.  
>   
> 7. On Survival Strategy  
>   
> Family and community are not just spiritual concepts; they are computable variables. They reduce the error term by providing shared information and pooled execution. Survival probability is a function of resource pooling and conflict resolution. There is no mystery here only computation.  
>   
> Final Test:  
>   
> I invite you to take your Three-Tier Model and translate it into a set of variables with a measurable outcome and a fixed time horizon. Run the experiment. Measure the error between your prediction and the outcome. Then, update the model.  
>   
> Until the math is applied, this remains a play entertaining, but non binding. I do not ask you to agree; I ask you to compute.  
>   
> JONA TENO

> The term “secret society” doesn't seem to me to be the right one to capture reality, as it often conjures up images of hoods and occult rituals, which allows critics to dismiss the idea as a conspiracy theory. However, these are often very real structures, based on power and financial relationships.
  
> So to describe these “interest groups” that operate behind the scenes (or in the open, but without transparency), here are the most precise terms used in political science and sociology:
  
> 1. Interest Convergence Groups
  
> This is the most neutral term. It describes individuals or organizations that do not necessarily have a formal structure, but whose financial or political interests align so perfectly that they act in concert. This is also referred to as “tacit coordination.”
  
> 2. Crony Capitalism
  
> This term refers to the close intertwining of business leaders and political officials. It is not “secret,” but it is a network of influence where decisions are made in private clubs, lodges, or closed-door boards of directors to favor a select group at the expense of the free market.
  
> 3. “Transnational Elites” or “Networks of Influence”
  
> Instead of “society,” the word “network” is used. A network has no single center nor a public membership list. It operates by co-optation. Organizations such as the World Economic Forum (WEF) or the Bilderberg Group fall into this category: they are not secret (they have websites), but their actual discussions and agreements are.
  
> 4. “Para-politics”
  
> This is a term used to describe networks that operate outside official democratic channels (intelligence agencies, industrial lobbies, major financial families) but dictate a country’s political direction.

>
> Yes, Texas is:   
>   
> “Open” = pro-growth, pro-business environment- No state income tax, Fewer regulatory barriers (relative to places like California), Easier for companies to relocate and expand, and Faster permitting / development timelines.  
>   
> “Cohesive” = cultural + political alignment  
> Strong shared values (religion, family, local identity). Less ideological fragmentation than coastal states (arguably). Community-centered lifestyle (churches, schools, local events)  
>   
> “Energy” = literal + economic power  
>   
> Texas is: The largest energy producer in the U.S. Huge in: Oil, Natural gas, Wind power (top in the country).  
>   
> So “energy” really means: Actual fuel + electricity, Economic leverage & Strategic importance  

> **@adamplace9449**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ue8y5e3HnHE&lc=UgyBh_BnfP_Q-OxTddZ4AaABAg.AVNv8UqBQ2jAVOaSkQaaI6)  
>
> As a current Texas resident, I think the situation is more complex than a few words  
> Texas is a beast that not many understand, and simply write it off as one thing or another   
>   
> The Texas population has 2 portions that are just about a equal in numbers: one that is connected to faith and family and wants to be left alone by the government, and one that is driven by a sense of social justice and a desire for shared prosperity  
> These two ends are not necessarily diametrically opposed youll notice  
>   
> Texas also has a government that is aligned with the power politics that we see being utilized by Donald trump, no tactic off the table if it brings the player closer to victory. They rely on creating a legal environment that is friendly to new business, specifically tech right now, while siphoning resources from different communities, using whatever boogeyman is most applicable at this moment  
>   
> Texans across the political spectrum are generally dissatisfied with this approach, yet many still participate, but the number of active participants is decreasing.  
>   
> People are concerned with “table talk” issues, like our energy grid and our economy. We want solutions that benefit us, and know that larger companies are getting those benefits and we’re getting screwed over by it  
>   
> Both portions of the population want things to be better, and are feeling frequently let down by the party apparatus of their choosing, but lack the motivation to break through the ceiling imposed by the power politic game players   
>   
> Both portions are internally cohesive for the most part, people have disagreements over finer details but generally want similar outcomes, and they are struggling to find commonalities by discussing first principles and looking at those desired outcomes outside of party approved talking points, but that’s been changing just from my personal experience (don’t know abt the larger trends)  
>   
> Texas has a whole lot of potential, which is why trump is looking at Texas to create more congressional seats, and why the maga machine is invested in Texas politics, keeping Abbott and other loyalists in power here  
>   
> Just my read of the situation though

### **@telegraphjames4542** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ue8y5e3HnHE&lc=Ugx1YSE4krOWfXZxMvJ4AaABAg)

Hey everyone, I've been following along with these videos like it seems a decent chunk of Youtube has. And while I think the analysis is good and fairly reasonable (even though nothing is reasonable about what is happening), I find that these videos leave me with a pit in my stomach. I journaled a bit about it and it was helpful for me. I'm going to post my journal entry below in the hopes that it will help other people who have similar feelings:

Yesterday, I listened to Professor Jiang's 'midterm exam' video, and found myself, while fixated on his fairly salient points, nauseated by the implications. And additionally, alienated and stressed out by his thinking in terms of game theory. While his thinking may be quite useful for bankers and despots, it seems to me of little use for the average person, unless they have no qualms about emptying their lives of any and all moral/ethical responsibility. The point that got me is this: his sugestion that one have children to survive the future, build community and affect creativity. I find these things to be utterly detached from each other: One can build community and find plenty of creativity and vigor without children, this is simply the professor's unimaginative and univocal/patriarchal voice speaking his values (no offense to the professor here). I digress. The point is that this got me thinking about how to transform the implications of his analysis into action. 
The crux of it is this: Professor Jiang's largely cogent worldview is rooted in a deep materialist cynicism. He posits that the material/military/economic vicissitudes of despotic empires and religious fanatics not only shape reality, but *are* reality. That the material is all that matters, exploded and extracted and manufactured to drive the world, utterly indifferent to human emotion, creativity, etc. And in a sense, he is right. The world is run by madmen. However, I believe that deriving any meaning or personal action plans from such a notion is morally bankrupt, reactionary, playing into the hands of the elites who shape the immanence of our material world. If we are to look at the guns and bombs that so catastrophically shape this world and base our actions off of their terrifying effects, then we will be no better than animals. We will have failed ourselves and the world. 
To think in these cynical materialist terms (as I have for much of my life, I can speak with experience here) will leave one frustrated, alienated, and viciously calculating in their personal life. To constantly be turning over the idea in one's head that the bombs and bullets of the material world invalidate the investment into the ethereal world of emotion, community, and human creativity is to do violence to oneself in their own life, and to the others around them by their callousness. To think in terms of game theory is to detach oneself from the palpable disgust one feels at the concepts of genocide, war, fraud, and violence more broadly. This disgust is a guiding force, and it is unwise to silence it for any long period of time, or as a character trait. Ignoring it will only lead us deeper into moral and personal abjection through our insistence that this media gives us any type of control through the pseudo-knowledge it imparts. 
Becasue at the end of the day, we are not game-masters, or grand agents, or members of the predatory and inhuman billionaire class. We are human. Flesh and bone and blood. Rooted in our place and beholden to forces beyond our control. If the elites are free agents moving about as they please, then we are simply the plants that they will trample so carelessly underfoot. We cannot live in fear of the boot that may one day trample us. We must find ways to flourish which accept the fragility of our existence, more apparent now than ever, and let the madmen rave. They will do so whether we love ourselves or not.

Anyways, no disrespect to the prof, but his interesting and engaging lectures make me feel like absolute poopy shit dog doodoo garbage. I hope my thoughts can help the scores of you out there that surely feel the same. This stuff is cool, but converting it into praxis means absolute surrender.

---

One moot point people use to bash him is calling all his stuff baseless and guessing (which it is, he faithfully disclaimed it many times). but as a former data science student who wrote hypotheses and do some statistical tests.... I was taught to start with "an educated guess" too. and guess what? that "educated guess" is dependent on me and the data available (either from a real dataset or artificial/simulation)


and even with all the rigidity and good practices, there are still loopholes like p-hacking (basically cheating your result), selective bias, imbalanced classes and all that stuff to skew the result. So it's not like the "traditional and highly objective" method is invulnerable to begin with.

I think we’re misreading this by focusing too much on the battlefield.

Iran is reacting in a fairly classical way, trying to gain leverage. But U.S. actions don’t always seem aimed purely at Iran and can look incoherent tactically because they operate on a different strategic layer.

With unclear objectives, we default to familiar models and assume limits still hold, while repeated escalation, de-escalation as some sort of spike and relief cycle, and constant nuclear framing keep shifting what feels thinkable.

So the real question is: what baseline is being movedand why, and what does that make possible?

Prof. Jiang's recent lectures carry a strong eschatological weight—there's a distinct shift from critical historical method toward end-world prophecy (His predictions are a schatological framework, so in his model those are working for something). While I deeply respect the depth of his analysis, I worry that the figure of the eschatological predictor can sometimes overshadow the material reality of the audience. When a compelling voice insists on a fixed, catastrophic endgame, it risks paralyzing agency rather than sharpening it.



My question is one of cui bono? Whose interests are served when the horizon is narrowed to an inevitable, predetermined collapse?(I invite you to think what is the most catastrophic of his predictions? who wins at the end? so they are the ones that boosts prof channel) I find myself longing for more of his rigorous historical critique—the kind that excavates the past to liberate the present—rather than the sealed architecture of a coming doom. I hope he returns to that ground soon.

### **@neutralneuralsun** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ue8y5e3HnHE&lc=UgyWB8x_5DWxtsIO-4d4AaABAg)

Professor Jiang, hope you see this message. I thoroughly enjoy your lectures and agree with most of your points (minor inaccuracies notwithstanding), especially regarding the western world.  I disagree with you on 2 points regarding the eastern world:
1. Japan will rise again as the most successful eastern asian country --- your historical evidence happened when China was at its weakest.  Given the calamities Japanese brought on to Chinese over the past 1 & 1/2 centuries, your prediction is not going to happen, because China will not allow it.
2. China is neither a civilization nor a nation-state, but rather a vassal/mirage state. As a STEM graduate, I find your assertion full of holes.  Attached is a rebuttal passage from "Lao Wu" a Youtuber who explains both why your point is wrong and how you would make such mistake.

> **@neutralneuralsun**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ue8y5e3HnHE&lc=UgyWB8x_5DWxtsIO-4d4AaABAg.AVWVL6VNcCBAVWVz3uXPgR)  
>
> Jiang Xueqin claims that China is neither a civilization nor a nation-state, but rather a vassal state constructed by an imperial system—an illusion.  
>   
> After hearing this, Lao Wu’s immediate reaction was that his understanding of China, to put it bluntly, is ignorant. He can dissect the American financial system, military logic, and the relationship between Hollywood and the Pentagon with remarkable clarity. Yet when it comes to China, he reduces it to such a simplistic and crude conclusion. I want to seriously explain where he goes wrong.  
>   
> First, he fundamentally does not understand what Chinese civilization is.  
>   
> Jiang left China at age six, grew up in Canada, studied English literature at Yale, and now teaches Western philosophy. The tools he uses to interpret the world are entirely Western frameworks. Applying Western imperial theory to China and concluding that China is an “illusion” doesn’t mean he has seen through China; it simply means his intellectual toolbox only contains one hammer, so everything looks like a nail to him.  
>   
> So, what exactly is Chinese civilization? It is the world’s only uninterrupted civilization, with a continuous history spanning over 5,000 years. Not “one of,” but the only one. Ancient Egyptian civilization perished. The Mesopotamian civilization perished. Ancient Indian civilization perished. Only Chinese civilization has never been broken. Archaeology tells us that as early as 5,300 years ago, the Liangzhu culture already possessed a complete state structure: palaces, city walls, large-scale water conservancy projects, and a strict ritual system and social hierarchy. This is tangible evidence that Chinese civilization was already highly developed while Western civilization was still in its early stages. Moreover, according to the latest archaeological research, the roots of Chinese civilization can be traced back 7,000 to 8,000 years, or even earlier. The Yangshao culture dates back approximately 7,000 years. Research led by Chinese archaeologist Feng Shi extends the lifeline of Chinese civilization to at least 8,000 years ago. We are not merely a 5,000-year-old civilization; we are an even older one.  
>   
> The continuity of Chinese civilization is evident in every detail. The language, writing system, rituals, and values we use today have a clear, traceable lineage connecting us to our ancestors thousands of years ago. This is not a system implanted by external forces. It is a civilized entity with deep roots and a distinct soul, grown organically from its own soil.  
>   
> Interestingly, even the most rigorous Western thinkers have had to acknowledge this. The British philosopher Bertrand Russell noted: “Since the time of Confucius, Egypt, Babylonia, Persia, Macedonia, and the Roman Empire have all perished, yet China has survived through continuous evolution.” The British historian Arnold Toynbee observed: “For millennia, China has succeeded more than any other nation in politically and culturally uniting hundreds of millions of people. This capability represents an unparalleled success.” Lao Wu cites these two Western thinkers not to prove that Chinese civilization needs Western validation, but to show that even Western observers who are most unfamiliar with China and most likely to hold biases clearly recognize its uniqueness and continuity. Meanwhile, Jiang Xueqin, an ethnic Chinese educator who has lived in China for years, calls China an “illusion manufactured by an imperial system.” This contrast alone speaks volumes.  
>   
> Second, his logical framework is fundamentally untenable.  
>   
> Jiang argues that China’s accession to the WTO and the massive outflow of students studying abroad are evidence of the imperial system replicating itself within China—a means of maintaining China as an “illusion.” This logic contains a fundamental flaw known in philosophy as unfalsifiability. What does that mean? Under his framework, if a country opens up and integrates into the global economy, it is “implanted by the imperial system” and becomes a vassal. If a country closes itself off and refuses integration, it is “isolated by the imperial system” and becomes a target for elimination. No matter what happens, his conclusion holds. A theory that can explain every possible scenario actually explains nothing. This is not analysis; it is faith. As Karl Popper stated, a sound theory must be falsifiable by facts. The fact that Jiang’s framework can never be wrong precisely proves it is fundamentally flawed.  
>   
> More importantly, Chinese civilization has, for millennia, possessed a tradition of absorbing foreign cultures and adapting them to its own needs. Buddhism came from India, yet China was not Indianized; instead, it Sinicized Buddhism. Marxism came from the West, yet China was not Westernized; instead, it Sinicized Marxism and forged its own path. This is not assimilation; it is a demonstration of Chinese civilization’s powerful digestive capacity. Openness has never meant losing subjectivity. Chinese civilization has proven this through thousands of years of history.  
>   
> Third, look at actions, not just theories.  
>   
> Jiang calls China a vassal state. Then let’s examine what a vassal state could possibly do.  
>   
> Start with the most difficult period in history. When the People’s Republic of China was founded, what was its condition? Penniless, devastated, with everything needing to be rebuilt from scratch. It had just endured a century of humiliation and warfare. The entire nation was essentially rising from the ashes. In that exact context, the Korean War broke out. The United States, leading UN forces, pushed right up to the Yalu River. Any normal vassal state would have bowed its head and compromised. China did not. Mao Zedong made the decision to resist America and aid Korea, sending an army that had just finished a civil war and was extremely poorly equipped to face the world’s most powerful military head-on. The result? They pushed UN forces back to the 38th Parallel, forced the United States to the negotiating table, and signed an armistice. This remains one of the most remarkable moments of sheer resilience in world history.  
>   
> Then came an even harder period: China simultaneously confronting two superpowers, the United States and the Soviet Union. On one side, Western blockade and sanctions; on the other, Soviet withdrawal of aid and political pressure. China bowed to neither. Later, China supported Vietnam in resisting American aggression, ultimately resulting in the United States withdrawing from Southeast Asia in disgrace. These events occurred during China’s most impoverished and vulnerable years. Could an “illusion without a soul” make such choices?  
>   
> Now, fast-forward to the present. In 2025, Trump launched a global tariff war, targeting every country in the world. Faced with this tariff cudgel, how did other nations react? Vietnam proactively offered zero tariffs on U.S. goods. Japan’s economic minister made seven trips to Washington, only to secure a 25% tariff rate—and actually celebrated that it wasn’t the previously threatened 30–35%. The European Union made concessions on automobiles and agricultural products in exchange for tariff relief. Canada and Mexico urgently called Trump, promising tighter border controls just to secure a temporary tariff pause. Trump even publicly used humiliating language to describe the negotiating countries, saying they were “sucking up” to him. Virtually all global leaders bowed to Trump in their own ways.  
>   
> Only China did not retreat a single step. For every round of U.S. tariffs, China responded with precise, proportionate countermeasures. No hesitation, no dragging feet. The exchange was firm and strategic. The final result? The United States backed down and came to the negotiating table with China. At the time, even mainstream Western media noted that without China as the exception, nearly the entire world had gradually bowed to Trump’s tariff pressure.  
>   
> Friends, these are not history books. This is reality we have witnessed with our own eyes. Could a vassal of an imperial system do any of this?  
>   
> Finally, a few words from Lao Wu.  
>   
> When Jiang Xueqin analyzes the United States and the Western system, many of his insights are valuable. Lao Wu has shared his content before, and that hasn’t changed. But his judgment on China is the weakest, most untenable part of his entire analytical framework. The reason, ultimately, is not complicated. He left China at age six. Armed with intellectual tools honed by Western literature and Western philosophy, he attempts to comprehend China without ever truly touching the historical depth of Chinese civilization, and without understanding the bone-deep conviction the Chinese people hold regarding sovereignty and dignity. That is the will forged through a century of humiliation: no matter how difficult it gets, we will never again be anyone’s vassal.  
>   
> China is a nation with a civilizational foundation spanning 5,000 years. Archaeology shows its roots may even stretch back 7,000 to 8,000 years. This civilization does not need anyone to grant it a soul, because its soul has been there all along—and it has never been interrupted.

### **@Breaking_Even**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ue8y5e3HnHE&lc=UgwKf5cztayIBE34RjN4AaABAg)

I don’t really agree on the cohesion and openness point when it comes to Texas. I’m with you 100% on the energy side though. The state has a huge advantage there with its natural resources, infrastructure, and rapid population growth. Looking at EIA data, in 2024 Texas accounted for about 43% of U.S. crude oil production and 28% of natural gas. It also leads the country in wind generation and ranks high in solar, while also being the largest energy consumer overall.

Where I push back is on cohesion and openness. Data from the Pew Research Center shows that trust among Americans has been declining over time. Back in 1972, about 46% of adults said most people can be trusted, but by 2018 that number dropped to 34%, based on the General Social Survey. I don’t really want to bring my personal experience or views into it, but that 34% figure is from 2018 and doesn’t account for the years since, and it’s not totally clear how they’re measuring or capturing that data over time.

That said, I still don’t think cohesion or openness in Texas is on the same level as New York City. From what I can tell, there’s more of both over there compared to Texas.

Hey can you do a video on 
The Tiananmen Square massacre refers to the bloody crackdown on pro-democracy protesters by the Chinese government on June 3–4, 1989, following weeks of demonstrations that began after the death of reformist leader Hu Yaobang in April 1989. 

Events and Crackdown
Martial law was declared on May 20, 1989, and by the early morning hours of June 4, troops and tanks moved into central Beijing, engaging in violent clashes with demonstrators along Chang'an Avenue and in the Muxidi suburbs, rather than solely within the square itself.  The government labeled the movement a "counter-revolutionary rebellion," and the crackdown resulted in the suppression of the protests, the exile or imprisonment of leaders, and the purge of officials like Zhao Ziyang. 

Casualty Estimates and Aftermath
The death toll remains disputed:

Official CCP tally: 241 dead. 
Independent estimates: Ranging from 300 to 2,700 dead. 
Western diplomats and media: Estimated hundreds to thousands killed, with up to 10,000 arrested.

The childfree community is not all composed of materialistic hedonists. There are those who opt not to have kids and pursue meaning in endeavors outside of caring for kids. There are those who don’t have the physical & mental condition to take care of them so they shouldn’t be shamed in not having them. Finally, there are those who don’t want to have kids for environmental reasons. Studies have shown that the most impactful action an individual can do other than being vegan & not riding a plane is to not have kids.

For Prof Jiang, it may provide meaning for him but that’s not the case for all people.
